asymptotically smooth and
\text{dist}(B_{v},B_{v^{\prime}})>0
, then

|(k,\sigma_{j}\otimes\sigma_{j^{\prime}})_{\Omega}|\leq C\dfrac{d^{2(q+1)}\text{diam}(v)^{q+1}\text{diam}(v^{\prime})^{q+1}}{(r\cdot\text{dist}(B_{v},B_{v^{\prime}}))^{2(q+1)}}\|\omega_{j}\|_{1}\|\omega_{j^{\prime}}\|_{1},

where
C(\Omega,k),r>0
are the constants from the asymptotically smoothness property.

Proof.

The Taylor expansion of
k
in
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in\Omega
is

k(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})=\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq q}\dfrac{(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}!}\dfrac{\partial^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|}}{\partial\boldsymbol{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}k(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{y})+\overbrace{\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|=q+1}\dfrac{(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}!}\dfrac{\partial^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|}}{\partial\boldsymbol{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}k(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})}^{R_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})},

where
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{x}_{0}+s(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0})
for some
s\in[0,1]
. The taylor expansion of
k
in
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in\Omega
allows writing

R_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})=\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|=q+1}\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\beta}|\leq q}\dfrac{(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{y}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}!}\dfrac{(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}!}\dfrac{\partial^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\boldsymbol{\beta}|}}{\partial\boldsymbol{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{y}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}k(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}_{0})+R_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{y}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})

with

R_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{y}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})=\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|,\boldsymbol{\beta}|=q+1}\dfrac{(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{y}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}!}\dfrac{(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}!}\dfrac{\partial^{2(q+1)}}{\partial\boldsymbol{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{y}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}k(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{y}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{y})),

where
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{y}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{y})=\boldsymbol{y}_{0}+t(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{y}_{0})
for some
t\in[0,1]
. We obtain a decomposition

k(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})=\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq q}f_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})b_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{y})+\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\beta}|\leq q}g_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{y})c_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{x})+R_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{y}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}),

where

f_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\dfrac{(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}!},\qquad g_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{y})=\dfrac{(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{y}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}!}

are polynomials of maximum degree
q
. With declaration
(i)
in Lemma
4.3.3
and the property that the samplets possess vanishing moments up to degree
q
, we receive

(k,\sigma_{j}\otimes\sigma_{j^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}=(R_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{y}_{0}},\sigma_{j}\otimes\sigma_{j^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}.

By using the asymptotically smoothness of the kernel and
(ii)
in Lemma
4.3.3
, we get

\displaystyle|(R_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{y}_{0}},\sigma_{j}

\displaystyle\otimes\sigma_{j^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dfrac{C}{r^{2(q+1)}}\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|,\boldsymbol{\beta}|=q+1}\dfrac{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\boldsymbol{\beta}|!}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}!\boldsymbol{\beta}!}\left(\dfrac{|(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{y}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|}{\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x})-\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{y}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{y})\|_{2}^{2(q+1)}},|\sigma_{j}|\otimes|\sigma_{j^{\prime}}|\right)_{\Omega\times\Omega}.

In the following we examine particular terms to receive the final upper bound. Let
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in B_{v}
and
\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in B_{v^{\prime}}
then are
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x})\in B_{v}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{y}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{y})\in B_{v^{\prime}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in v,\boldsymbol{y}\in v^{\prime}}\,\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x})-\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{y}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{y})\|_{2}^{-2(q+1)}\leq\text{dist}(B_{v},B_{v^{\prime}})^{-2(q+1)}.

Next is

\displaystyle\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|,\boldsymbol{\beta}|=q+1}\dfrac{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\boldsymbol{\beta}|!}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}!\boldsymbol{\beta}!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|,\boldsymbol{\beta}|=q+1}\binom{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\boldsymbol{\beta}|}{|\boldsymbol{\beta}|}\dfrac{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|!|\boldsymbol{\beta}|!}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}!\boldsymbol{\beta}!}

\displaystyle=\binom{2(q+1)}{q+1}\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|,\boldsymbol{\beta}|=q+1}\dfrac{(q+1)!(q+1)!}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}!\boldsymbol{\beta}!}

\displaystyle\leq 4^{q+1}\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|,\boldsymbol{\beta}|=q+1}\dfrac{(q+1)!(q+1)!}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}!\boldsymbol{\beta}!}=4^{q+1}d^{2(q+1)},

where we used

\sum_{n=0}^{2(q+1)}\binom{2(q+1)}{n}=(1+1)^{2(q+1)}=4^{q+1}

for the first inequality and the formula

d^{q+1}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}1\right)^{q+1}=\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|=q+1}\dfrac{(q+1)!}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}!},

provided by the multinomial theorem, for the last equality. Furthermore

\displaystyle(|(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|,|\sigma_{j}|)_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\omega_{j,i}||(\boldsymbol{x}_{i}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\omega_{j,i}|\left(\dfrac{\text{diam}(v)}{2}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|}=\dfrac{\text{diam}(v)^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|}}{2^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|}}\|\omega_{j}\|_{1},

where the inequality follows by choosing
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
as
B_{v}
’s midpoint, i. e.

\boldsymbol{x}_{0}=\left(\max_{\boldsymbol{x}\in v}\,x_{1}/2+\min_{\boldsymbol{y}\in v}\,y_{1}/2,...,\max_{\boldsymbol{x}\in v}\,x_{d}/2+\min_{\boldsymbol{y}\in v}\,y_{d}/2\right)^{T}.

By combining all these inequalities and using
(i)
,
(iii)
of Lemma
4.3.3
, we receive

\displaystyle|(k,\sigma_{j}

\displaystyle\otimes\sigma_{j^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}|

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{C}{r^{2(q+1)}}4^{q+1}d^{2(q+1)}\text{dist}(B_{v},B_{v^{\prime}})^{-2(q+1)}\dfrac{\text{diam}(v)^{q+1}\text{diam}(v^{\prime})^{q+1}}{2^{q+1}2^{q+1}}\|\omega_{j}\|_{1}\|\omega_{j^{\prime}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=C\dfrac{d^{2(q+1)}\text{diam}(v)^{q+1}\text{diam}(v^{\prime})^{q+1}}{(r\cdot\text{dist}(B_{v},B_{v^{\prime}}))^{2(q+1)}}\|\omega_{j}\|_{1}\|\omega_{j^{\prime}}\|_{1}\qed

Let
\boldsymbol{T}
be the change of basis matrix from samplet basis
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{J}=\{\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{N}\}
to canonical basis. Using statement
(iv)
of Lemma
4.3.3
, we define the kernel matrix in samplet representation as

\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}:=\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{K}\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}=[(k,\sigma_{j}\otimes\sigma_{j^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}]_{1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq N}.

We compress the matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
by setting its small entries to zero through thresholding. Directly calculating all entries only to zero out most of them would be computationally expensive, so we seek for a simple criterion to determine which entries to skip. For
\eta>0
and two clusters
v
and
v^{\prime}
, we consider the admissibility condition

\text{dist}(B_{v},B_{v^{\prime}})\geq\eta\max\{\text{diam}(B_{v}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})\}.

(4.3.3)

This inequality is straightforward to check since for
B_{v}=\mathop{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{\hbox{\LARGE{$\times$}}}}_{i=1}^{d}[a_{v,i},b_{v,i}]
is

\text{diam}(B_{v})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}(b_{v,i}-a_{v,i})^{2}\right)^{1/2}

and

\text{dist}(B_{v},B_{v^{\prime}})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\max\{0,a_{v,i}-b_{v^{\prime},i}\}^{2}+\max\{0,a_{v^{\prime},i}-b_{v,i}\}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Storing the bounding boxes for each cluster in Algorithm
2
ensures that computing these values for a single cluster has a constant cost. However, with
\mathcal{O}(N)
clusters, the overall computation cost would be
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
. Later, we will demonstrate that the actual computational cost is lower, specifically
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
.

The following Theorem, with its proof provided by
[
14
]
, demonstrates that the compression strategy is decent, with the parameter
\eta
determining the extent of compression. For the remainder of this chapter, we adopt the same settings as in Proposition
4.3.4
. Additionally, we require that the cluster tree is sufficiently deep by setting the threshold to less than
m_{q}+1
, allowing us to apply Lemma
4.2.9
and ensuring that no cluster provides more than
m_{q}
samplets.

Theorem 4.3.5
.

Let
(X_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of points with
q_{X_{N}}\geq C_{1}N^{-1/d}
for some constant
C_{1}(\Omega)>0
and set all coefficients of

\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=[(k,\sigma_{j}\otimes\sigma_{j^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}]_{1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq N}

to zero that satisfy the admissibility condition (
4.3.3
). Then the compressed matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
satisfies

\|\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\|_{F}\leq C\eta^{-2(q+1)}N\sqrt{\log N}

for some constant
C(\Omega,k,q)>0
, where
\|\cdot\|_{F}
denotes the Frobenius norm.

Proof.

Let
\sigma_{j,i}
,
\sigma_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}
be two samplets with support in clusters
v
,
v^{\prime}
, coefficient vectors
\omega_{j,i}
,
\omega_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}
and that satisfy condition (
4.3.3
). After Proposition
4.3.4
is

\displaystyle|(k,\sigma_{j,i}\otimes\sigma_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}})_{\Omega}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\dfrac{d^{2(q+1)}\text{diam}(v)^{q+1}\text{diam}(v^{\prime})^{q+1}}{(r\cdot\text{dist}(B_{v},B_{v^{\prime}}))^{2(q+1)}}\|\omega_{j,i}\|_{1}\|\omega_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\dfrac{d^{2(q+1)}\text{diam}(B_{v})^{q+1}\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})^{q+1}}{(r\cdot\text{dist}(B_{v},B_{v^{\prime}}))^{2(q+1)}}\|\omega_{j,i}\|_{1}\|\omega_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\dfrac{d^{2(q+1)}\min\{\text{diam}(B_{v}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})\}^{q+1}}{(\eta r)^{2(q+1)}\max\{\text{diam}(B_{v}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})\}^{q+1}}\|\omega_{j,i}\|_{1}\|\omega_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\left(\dfrac{d}{\eta r}\right)^{2(q+1)}\theta_{(j,i),(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})}\|\omega_{j,i}\|_{1}\|\omega_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}\|_{1},

where
C_{2}(\Omega,k),r>0
are constants and we denote

\theta_{(j,i),(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})}=\dfrac{\min\{\text{diam}(B_{v}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})\}}{\max\{\text{diam}(B_{v}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})\}}\leq 1.

For
j\in\{0,...,J-1\}
let
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j}=\{\sigma_{j,1},...,\sigma_{j,|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j}|}\}
be the set of samplets that the clusters on level
j
provide. There are
2^{j}
clusters on this level, each of them provides not more than
m_{q}
samplets and the amount of elements in a cluster is on average
N/2^{j}
. Using these facts and statement
4
of Theorem
4.2.13
yields

\sum_{i=1}^{|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j}|}\|\omega_{j,i}\|_{1}^{2}\theta_{(j,i),(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})}^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j}|}|v_{j,i}|\theta_{(j,i),(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{2^{j}m_{q}}\dfrac{N}{2^{j}}\theta_{(j,i),(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})}=\dfrac{N}{2^{j}}\sum_{i=1}^{2^{j}m_{q}}\theta_{(j,i),(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})},

where
\sigma_{j,i}
has support in cluster
v_{j,i}
on level
j
. Using statements
1
and
2
of Proposition
4.2.6
, we receive

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2^{j}m_{q}}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{2^{j^{\prime}}m_{q}}\theta_{(j,i),(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=m_{q}^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\text{ is a cluster on level }j\\
v^{\prime}\text{ is a cluster on level }j^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\min\{\text{diam}(B_{v}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})\}}{\max\{\text{diam}(B_{v}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})\}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\text{ is a cluster on level }j\\
v^{\prime}\text{ is a cluster on level }j^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\min\{\text{diam}(B_{v}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})\}}{N^{1/d}q_{X_{N}}\cdot\max\{2^{-j/d},2^{-j^{\prime}/d}\}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\dfrac{\min\{2^{j^{\prime}}2^{j-j/d},2^{j}2^{j^{\prime}-j^{\prime}/d}\}}{\max\{2^{-j/d},2^{-j^{\prime}/d}\}}=2^{j+j^{\prime}}\dfrac{\min\{2^{-j/d},2^{-j^{\prime}/d}\}}{\max\{2^{-j/d},2^{-j^{\prime}/d}\}},

where the constants in
\lesssim
depend on
\Omega
and
q
. We further use the estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{J-1}\dfrac{\min\{2^{-j/d},2^{-j^{\prime}/d}\}}{\max\{2^{-j/d},2^{-j^{\prime}/d}\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j}\dfrac{2^{-j/d}}{2^{-j^{\prime}/d}}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=j+1}^{J-1}\dfrac{2^{-j^{\prime}/d}}{2^{-j/d}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j}2^{-j^{\prime}/d}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{J-j-1}2^{-j^{\prime}/d}\leq 2\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j^{\prime}/d}=\dfrac{2}{1-2^{-1/d}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(j,i)\text{ and }(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})\text{ satisfy }(\ref{eq. 4.3.15})}\sum_{i=1}^{|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j}|}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j^{\prime}}|}|(k,\sigma_{j,i}\otimes\sigma_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}})_{\Omega}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}^{2}\left(\dfrac{d}{\eta r}\right)^{4(q+1)}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=0}^{J-1}\sum_{i=1}^{|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j}|}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j^{\prime}}|}\theta_{(j,i),(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})}^{2}\|\omega_{j,i}\|_{1}^{2}\|\omega_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}\|_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}^{2}\left(\dfrac{d}{\eta r}\right)^{4(q+1)}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=0}^{J-1}\dfrac{N^{2}}{2^{j+j^{\prime}}}\sum_{i=1}^{2^{j}m_{q}}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{2^{j^{\prime}}m_{q}}\theta_{(j,i),(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\eta^{-4(q+1)}N^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{J-1}\dfrac{\min\{2^{-j/d},2^{-j^{\prime}/d}\}}{\max\{2^{-j/d},2^{-j^{\prime}/d}\}}\lesssim\eta^{-4(q+1)}N^{2}J,

where the constants in
\lesssim
depend on
\Omega,k
and
q
. Note that the root cluster does not satisfy (
4.3.3
) with respect to any other cluster, so the scaling functions within
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{J}
do not need to be considered. The proof of Proposition
4.2.4
states that the total amount of levels is not more than

\bigg\lceil\dfrac{\log(N/2)}{\log 2}\bigg\rceil+1=\bigg\lceil\dfrac{\log N-\log 2}{\log 2}\bigg\rceil+1=\bigg\lceil\dfrac{\log N}{\log 2}\bigg\rceil<2\log N.

Taking this into account, we have

\|\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\|_{F}\leq C\eta^{-2(q+1)}N\sqrt{\log N}

for a constant
C(\Omega,k,q)>0
.
∎

A direct consequence is the following corollary, which bounds the relative error of the compressed matrix.

Corollary 4.3.6
.

Let
(X_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of points that satisfies
q_{X_{N}}\geq C_{1}N^{-1/d}
and let
\|\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{F}\geq C_{2}N
for some constants
C_{1}(\Omega),C_{2}(\overline{\Omega},k)>0
. Then

\dfrac{\|\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\|_{F}}{\|\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{F}}\leq C\eta^{-2(q+1)}\sqrt{\log N},

where
C(\overline{\Omega},k,q)>0
is a constant.

As the Matérn kernels are of interest in my thesis, it is important to note that they satisfy the condition to bound the relative error.

Lemma 4.3.7
.

For a Matérn kernel exists
C(\Omega,k)>0
with
\|\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{F}\geq CN
.

Proof.

The Matérn kernel
k_{\nu,\ell}
is continuous on
\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{\Omega}
and positive, implying there exists
C=C(\overline{\Omega},k)>0
such that
k_{\nu,\ell}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\geq C
for all
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\Omega
. Now,

\|\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{F}^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{K}\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\|_{F}^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{K}\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}k_{\nu,\ell}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{x}_{j})^{2}\geq\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}C^{2}=N^{2}C^{2}.\qed

For an appropriate experimental design, the compressed matrix is sparse. This ensures that our compression strategy is effective.

Proposition 4.3.8
.

Let
(X_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of quasi-uniform points in
\Omega
. The compressed matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
has
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
non-zero entries.

Proof.

Let
v_{j,i}
be the
i
-th cluster at level
j
and let
L_{\ell}
be the length of
B_{v_{j,i}}
’s edge along dimension
\ell
. According to Corollary
4.2.7
and the proof of statement
1
in Proposition
4.2.6
, there exists a constant
C_{1}(\Omega)>0
such that

L_{\ell}\geq C_{1}2^{-j/d}\quad\text{for every }\ell\in\{1,...,d\}.

According to Corollary
4.2.7
, there is also a constant
C_{2}(\Omega)>0
such that

\text{diam}(B_{v_{j,i}})\leq C_{1}C_{2}2^{-j/d}.

This means condition (
4.3.3
) becomes at worst

\text{dist}(B_{v_{j,i}},B_{v_{j,i^{\prime}}})\geq\eta C_{1}C_{2}2^{-j/d}.

This condition fails for at most
3^{d}\eta^{d}C_{2}^{d}
clusters at level
j
, as illustrated in the accompanying graphic:

Taking this into account and noting that
v_{j,i^{\prime}}
has
2^{j^{\prime}-j}
child clusters at level
j^{\prime}
, there are
\mathcal{O}(3^{d}\eta^{d}C_{2}^{d}2^{j^{\prime}-j})=\mathcal{O}(2^{j^{\prime}-j})
clusters at level
j^{\prime}
that do not satisfy the admissibility condition. Since each cluster contains at most
m_{q}
samplets, the number of non-thresholded entries in the symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
is at most

\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{J}2^{j}\sum_{j^{\prime}\geq j}2^{j^{\prime}-j}m_{q}^{2}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{J}\sum_{j^{\prime}\geq j}2^{j^{\prime}}\right)

In the proof of Proposition
4.2.4
, we have established
J=\mathcal{O}(\log N)
, and clearly we have
2^{J}=\mathcal{O}(N)
clusters at the last level. Using the identity

\sum_{j=0}^{J}\sum_{j^{\prime}\geq j}2^{j^{\prime}}=\begin{array}[]{rrr}2^{0}+2^{1}+...+2^{J}\\
+2^{1}+...+2^{J}\\
\vdots\\
+2^{J}\end{array}=\sum_{j=0}^{J}(j+1)2^{j},

we further derive

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{J}\sum_{j^{\prime}\geq j}2^{j^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{J}(j+1)2^{j}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left((J+1)2^{J}\sum_{j=0}^{J}2^{-j}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(J2^{J}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(J2^{J}\dfrac{1}{1-1/2}\right)=\mathcal{O}(N\log N).

This implies that
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
entries in
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
are not thresholded.
∎

It follows an example, where we visualize the sparsity pattern of a compressed kernel matrix.

Example 4.3.9
.

Consider
N=10,000
equispaced points in
(0,1)^{2}
with a step size of
0.01
in each dimension,
q=3
vanishing moments,
\eta=0.8
and the Matérn kernel
k_{3/2,1}
. The kernel matrix
\boldsymbol{K}
is dense, while the sparse matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
is visualized in the left panel of Figure
16
and has a relative compression error of
1.8737\cdot 10^{-5}
.

If we additionally threshold entries smaller than
10^{-6}
, we obtain an even sparser matrix. It is visualized in the right panel of Figure
16
, with a relative compression error of
1.4865\cdot 10^{-5}
. Since the difference between the errors is negligible, it is beneficial to further threshold small entries of
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
. For more details on the sparsity pattern, refer to the original work
[
14
]
.

Figure 16:
The left panel displays
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
as described in Example
4.3.9
, while the right panel shows
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
with entries smaller than
10^{-6}
thresholded.

4.4
Computation of the compressed kernel matrix

A straightforward computation of the compressed kernel matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
would be too expensive, as it would involve calculating
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\boldsymbol{TK}\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}
and then checking the admissibility condition (
4.3.3
) for every pair of clusters
(v,v^{\prime})
. Instead, our setting enables the direct computation of
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
using
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-matrix techniques. Specifically, we recursively compute the blocks of this matrix, where each block corresponds to a pair of cluster
(v,v^{\prime})
. The next lemma demonstrates that certain blocks can be skipped, and in conjunction with Proposition
4.3.8
, we conclude that for quasi-uniform points only
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
blocks need to be computed. Since each block will require a constant amount of computational effort, this provides an efficient strategy for computing
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
.

Lemma 4.4.1
.

Let the clusters
v
and
v^{\prime}
satisfy criterion (
4.3.3
). Then for son clusters
v_{son}
of
v
and
v^{\prime}_{son}
of
v^{\prime}
is:

(i)

\text{dist}(B_{v_{son}},B_{v^{\prime}})\geq\eta\max\{\text{diam}(B_{v_{son}}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})\}
,

(ii)

\text{dist}(B_{v_{son}},B_{v^{\prime}_{son}})\geq\eta\max\{\text{diam}(B_{v_{son}}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}_{son}})\}
.

Proof.

The bounding box
B_{v_{son}}
lies within
B_{v}
and therefore

\displaystyle\text{dist}(B_{v_{son}},B_{v^{\prime}})\geq\text{dist}(B_{v},B_{v^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\geq\eta\max\{\text{diam}(B_{v}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})\}

\displaystyle\geq\eta\max\{\text{diam}(B_{v_{son}}),\text{diam}(B_{v^{\prime}})\}.

We get
(ii)
by using
(i)
twice.
∎

Let
\{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}^{v},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}^{v},...,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}^{v}\}\subset\Omega
be a set of interpolation points for the cluster
v
, where the components satisfy
\xi_{i,j}^{v}\neq\xi_{i,j^{\prime}}^{v}
for
j\neq j^{\prime}
. Define the corresponding tensor Lagrange polynomials as

\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v}(\boldsymbol{x})=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\prod_{i\neq\alpha_{j}}^{n}\dfrac{x_{j}-\xi_{i,j}^{v}}{\xi_{\alpha_{j},j}^{v}-\xi_{i,j}^{v}}\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{1,2,...,n\}^{d}

(see section B.3.2 in
[
13
]
). For an admissible pair of clusters
(v,v^{\prime})
, we approximate the kernel
k
via interpolation

k(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\approx\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\{1,2,...,n\}^{d}}\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v}(\boldsymbol{x})k(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v^{\prime}})\ell_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{y})\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{x}\in B_{v},\boldsymbol{y}\in B_{v^{\prime}},

where

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v}=(\xi_{\alpha_{1},1}^{v},\xi_{\alpha_{2},2}^{v},...,\xi_{\alpha_{d},d}^{v}).

This approximation is used only for admissible pairs of clusters, as the smoothness of the kernel is required for a good approximation, which can only be guaranteed when
\boldsymbol{x}\neq\boldsymbol{y}
. Specifically, the asymptotic smoothness property from Definition
4.3.1
suggests that the polynomial approximation improves as
\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\|
increases.

Let
\varphi_{j}^{v},\varphi_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}\in V
have coefficient vectors
[\omega_{j,i}]_{i=1}^{N},[\omega_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}]_{i^{\prime}=1}^{N}
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and support in clusters
v,v^{\prime}
. Then is

\displaystyle(k,\varphi_{j}^{v}\otimes\varphi_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\omega_{j,i}\omega_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}(k,\delta_{\boldsymbol{x}_{i}}\otimes\delta_{\boldsymbol{x}_{i^{\prime}}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{s=1}^{N}\sum_{s^{\prime}=1}^{N}\omega_{j,i}\omega_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\{1,2,...,n\}^{d}}\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i})k(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v^{\prime}})\ell_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\{1,2,...,n\}^{d}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\omega_{j,i}\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i})\right)k(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v^{\prime}})\left(\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\omega_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}\ell_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i^{\prime}})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\{1,2,...,n\}^{d}}(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\varphi_{j}^{v})_{\Omega}k(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v^{\prime}})(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v^{\prime}},\varphi_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega}.

This implies that if
(v,v^{\prime})
is admissible, we approximate a matrix block via

\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}:=[(k,\varphi_{j,i}^{v}\otimes\varphi_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}]_{1\leq i\leq|\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}|,1\leq i^{\prime}\leq|\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}|}\approx\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}\boldsymbol{S}_{v,v^{\prime}}\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{T},

where the matrices are defined as

\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}=[(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\varphi_{j,i}^{v})_{\Omega}]_{1\leq i\leq|\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}|,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{S}_{v,v^{\prime}}=[k(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v^{\prime}})]_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}.

Similarly, we approximate the matrix block

\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}:=[(k,\sigma_{j,i}^{v}\otimes\sigma_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}]_{1\leq i\leq|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j}^{v}|,1\leq i^{\prime}\leq|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}|}\approx\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v}\boldsymbol{S}_{v,v^{\prime}}\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{T},

even though we only require this matrix block for recursive calculations. Using the same notations as in sections
4.2.2
and
4.2.4
, we can also express

\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v}\right)^{T}=[(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j}^{v})_{\Omega}]_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}\quad\text{and}\quad\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}\right)^{T}=[(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v})_{\Omega}]_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}.

The following lemma demonstrates, that this basis matrices can be calculated recursively.

Lemma 4.4.2
.

For a child cluster
v_{\text{son}}
of
v
define
\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}}}=[\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v_{\text{son}}})]_{\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}
. Then it holds

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v}\end{bmatrix}=\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}\right)^{T}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}1}}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}2}}\end{bmatrix},

where
\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}
is the transformation matrix from section
4.2.2
.

Proof.

The Lagrange polynomials belong to the subspace

\text{span}\big\{z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdot...\cdot z_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}\ |\ \alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},...,\alpha_{d}\in\{0,1,...,n-1\}\big\}\subset\mathcal{P}_{nd}(\Omega)

(4.4.1)

which has dimension
n^{d}
. Using the identity

\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v})=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\beta}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(4.4.2)

it is straightforward to demonstrate that the Lagrange polynomials are linearly independent. Given that there are
n^{d}
Lagrange polynomials, they form a basis for the subspace defined in (
4.4.1
). Therefore, any
\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v}
can be represented using the Lagrange polynomials
\ell_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v_{\text{son}}}
of one of its child clusters
v_{\text{son}}
. By utilizing equation (
4.4.2
) for the child cluster
v_{\text{son}}
, the coefficients for this representation can be easily determined, leading to the expression

\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v}(\boldsymbol{x})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v_{\text{son}}})\ell_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v_{\text{son}}}(\boldsymbol{x})\quad\text{for all }\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega.

Specifically, it holds

\displaystyle[(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j+1}^{v_{\text{son}}})_{\Omega}]_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}

\displaystyle=[\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v_{\text{son}}})]_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}\cdot[(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v_{\text{son}}},\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j+1}^{v_{\text{son}}})_{\Omega}]_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}}}\right)^{T}.

Utilizing this equality and equation (
4.2.6
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v}\end{bmatrix}^{T}=\left[\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}\right)^{T}\big|\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v}\right)^{T}\right]

\displaystyle=[(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v})_{\Omega}\,|\,(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j}^{v})_{\Omega}]_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}

\displaystyle=[(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j+1}^{v})_{\Omega}]_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}

\displaystyle=[(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j+1}^{v_{\text{son}1}})_{\Omega}\,|\,(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j+1}^{v_{\text{son}2}})_{\Omega}]_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}

\displaystyle=\left[\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}1}}\right)^{T}\big|\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}2}}\right)^{T}\right]\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}1}}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}2}}\end{bmatrix}^{T}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}.\qed

The procedure to compute the basis matrices is formulated in Algorithm
6
. Define

\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}=(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}=[(k,\varphi_{j,i}^{v}\otimes\sigma_{j^{\prime},i^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}]_{1\leq i\leq|\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}|,1\leq i^{\prime}\leq|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}|},

then for an admissible pair
(v,v^{\prime})
, we are now able to approximate

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}\approx\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{S}_{v,v^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{T}&\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{T}\end{bmatrix}.

To compute the non-admissible matrix blocks, we use the refinement relations stated in the next lemma.

Algorithm 6

Multiscale cluster basis matrices

Data:

Cluster tree
\mathcal{T}
, cluster
v
,
(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v})_{v\in P\backslash\mathcal{L(T)}}
,
(\boldsymbol{V}_{v}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}})_{v\in\mathcal{L(T)}}
,
(\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}}})_{v\in P\backslash\mathcal{L(T)}}

Result:

Multiscale cluster basis matrices
(\boldsymbol{V}_{v}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}})_{v\in P}
and
(\boldsymbol{V}_{v}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})_{v\in P\backslash\mathcal{L(T)}}

Function

computeMultiscaleClusterBasis(
v
)
:

if

children
v_{\text{son}1},v_{\text{son}2}
of
v
aren’t leaf clusters

then

\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1}}=\texttt{computeMultiscaleClusterBasis}(v_{\text{son}1})

\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2}}=\texttt{computeMultiscaleClusterBasis}(v_{\text{son}2})

end if

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v}\end{bmatrix}=\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}\right)^{T}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}1}}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}2}}\end{bmatrix}

store
(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v},\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v})

return

\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}

end

\texttt{computeMultiscaleClusterBasis}(X)

Lemma 4.4.3
.

We have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}=\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}\right)^{T}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}.

Proof.

It is

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}&(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}\otimes[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}]\right)_{\Omega\times\Omega}=\left(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}\otimes(\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}+1}^{v^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})\right)_{\Omega\times\Omega}

\displaystyle=\left(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}+1}^{v^{\prime}}\right)_{\Omega\times\Omega}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}=\left(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}\otimes\left[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}+1}^{v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}+1}^{v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}\right]\right)_{\Omega\times\Omega}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}+1}^{v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}&(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}+1}^{v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}},

where the fourth equality can be verified through direct calculation. Similarly, we get

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}.

Also is

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}\\
(k,\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j}^{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}\end{bmatrix}=\left(k,[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j}^{v}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j}^{v}]\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}\right)_{\Omega\times\Omega}=\left(k,(\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j+1}^{v}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v})\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}+1}^{v^{\prime}}\right)_{\Omega\times\Omega}

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}\right)^{T}\left(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j+1}^{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}+1}^{v^{\prime}}\right)_{\Omega\times\Omega}=\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}\right)^{T}\left(k,[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j+1}^{v_{\text{son}1}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j+1}^{v_{\text{son}2}}]\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}+1}^{v^{\prime}}\right)_{\Omega\times\Omega}

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}\right)^{T}\begin{bmatrix}(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j+1}^{v_{\text{son}1}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}\\
(k,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j+1}^{v_{\text{son}2}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}\end{bmatrix}=\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}\right)^{T}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix},

where the fourth equality can be verified through direct calculation. Similarly, we get

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}=\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}\right)^{T}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}.\qed

Algorithm
7
outlines the recursive process for calculating a matrix block. It can be applied to compute
\boldsymbol{K}^{\Sigma}_{\eta}
, as described in
[
14
]
. However, to avoid nested functions, I formulate the computation of
\boldsymbol{K}^{\Sigma}_{\eta}
without using
\texttt{recursivelyDetermineBlock}(v,v^{\prime})
.

Algorithm 7

Compute matrix block

Data:

Cluster tree
\mathcal{T}
,
(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v})_{v\in P\backslash\mathcal{L(T)}}
,
(\boldsymbol{V}_{v}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}})_{v\in P}
,
(\boldsymbol{V}_{v}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})_{v\in P\backslash\mathcal{L(T)}}
,
\{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}^{v},...,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}^{v}\}_{v\in P}

Result:

Matrix block
\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}
for a pair of clusters
(v,v^{\prime})

Function

recursivelyDetermineBlock(
v,v^{\prime}
)
:

if

(v,v^{\prime})
is admissible

then

\boldsymbol{S}_{v,v^{\prime}}=[k(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{v},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{v^{\prime}})]_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\{1,...,n\}^{d}}

return

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{S}_{v,v^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{T}&\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{T}\end{bmatrix}

else if

v,v^{\prime}
are leaf clusters

then

return

\boldsymbol{K}_{v,v^{\prime}}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}=[(k,\delta_{\boldsymbol{x}}\otimes\delta_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}]_{\boldsymbol{x}\in v,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in v^{\prime}}

else if

v
is a leaf cluster

then

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}\end{bmatrix}=\texttt{recursivelyDetermineBlock}(v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1})

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}\end{bmatrix}=\texttt{recursivelyDetermineBlock}(v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2})

return

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}

else

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}=\texttt{recursivelyDetermineBlock}(v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime})

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}=\texttt{recursivelyDetermineBlock}(v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime})

return

\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}\right)^{T}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}

end

end

Algorithm
8
finalizes the computation of
\boldsymbol{K}^{\Sigma}_{\eta}
by sequentially calculating each column, starting with the last column and moving from bottom to top, then continuing in the same manner for the second-to-last column, and so forth, until the first column is finished. During the computation of a column, admissible blocks are skipped, and previously computed blocks to the right and below are reused. The admissible matrix blocks are calculated only if necessary. The coupling matrices
\boldsymbol{S}_{v,v^{\prime}}
are calculated on the fly as in Algorithm
7
, but for the sake of brevity I have omitted this detail in the notation of Algorithm
8
. This algorithm enables efficient computation of
\boldsymbol{K}^{\Sigma}_{\eta}
while minimizing storage requirements.

Proposition 4.4.4
.

Let
(X_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of quasi-uniform points in
\Omega
. The computational workload of computing
\boldsymbol{K}^{\Sigma}_{\eta}
using Algorithms
6
and
8
is
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
, with the storage requirements also being
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.2.4
, we can construct a balanced binary tree
\mathcal{T}=(P,E)
in
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
time and by Proposition
4.2.12
, we can compute
(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v})_{v\in P\backslash\mathcal{L(T)}}
in
\mathcal{O}(N)
time. For non-leaf clusters, the matrix
\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}}}
requires only constant time to compute and for leaf clusters, the matrix
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}
also requires constant time. Therefore, gathering the inputs for Algorithm
6
takes
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
time. For a non-leaf cluster, the calculation

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v}\end{bmatrix}=\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}\right)^{T}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}1}}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{v,v_{\text{son}2}}\end{bmatrix}

also requires constant time. Given that there are
\mathcal{O}(N)
clusters, the execution of Algorithm
6
takes
\mathcal{O}(N)
time.

The computation of all coupling matrices
(\boldsymbol{S}_{v,v^{\prime}})_{v,v^{\prime}\in P}
would take
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
time, but since we compute only the necessary ones on the fly while calculating non-admissible matrix blocks, and by Proposition
4.3.8
there are
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
non-admissible matrix blocks, the computation of these coupling matrices takes
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
time.

When executing Algorithm
8
, we only calculate
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
matrix blocks, as admissible blocks are excluded. Determining whether a pair of clusters is admissible takes constant time, and using Lemma
4.4.1
we limit the checks to
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
clusters. Since the involved multiplications for each matrix block take constant time, the execution of Algorithm 8 also takes
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
time. In summary, we can compute
\boldsymbol{K}^{\Sigma}_{\eta}
in
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
time. Similarly, we derive that the storage requirements are
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
.
∎

Algorithm 8

Computation of the compressed kernel matrix

Data:

Cluster tree
\mathcal{T}
,
(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v})_{v\in P\backslash\mathcal{L(T)}}
,
(\boldsymbol{V}_{v}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}})_{v\in P}
,
(\boldsymbol{V}_{v}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})_{v\in P\backslash\mathcal{L(T)}}
,
\{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}^{v},...,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{n}^{v}\}_{v\in P}

Result:

Sparse matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}

Function

setupColumn(
v^{\prime}
)
:

foreach

son
v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}
of
v^{\prime}

do

\texttt{setupColumn}(v^{\prime}_{\text{son}})

end foreach

\texttt{setupRow}(X,v^{\prime})

end

Function

setupRow(
v,v^{\prime}
)
:

if

v
is not a leaf

then

foreach

son
v_{\text{son}}
of
v

do

if

(v_{\text{son}},v^{\prime})
is non-admissible

then

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}},v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}},v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}=\texttt{setupRow}(v_{\text{son}},v^{\prime})

else

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}},v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}},v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}}}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}}}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{S}_{v_{\text{son}},v^{\prime}}\begin{bmatrix}\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{T}&\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{T}\end{bmatrix}

end if

end foreach

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}=\left(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v}\right)^{T}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}1},v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v_{\text{son}2},v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}

else if

v^{\prime}
is a leaf cluster

then

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}=[(k,\delta_{\boldsymbol{x}}\otimes\delta_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}})_{\Omega\times\Omega}]_{\boldsymbol{x}\in v,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in v^{\prime}}

else

foreach

son
v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}
of
v^{\prime}

do

if

(v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}})
is non-admissible

then

load already computed block
\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}}\end{bmatrix}

else

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{S}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}}\begin{bmatrix}\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}}\right)^{T}&\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v^{\prime}_{\text{son}}}\right)^{T}\end{bmatrix}

end if

end foreach

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}1}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}_{\text{son}2}}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{Q}_{j^{\prime}}^{v^{\prime}}

end

store
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}
in
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
and
return

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\\
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{v,v^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{v,v^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}

end

Initialize
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
as
N\times N
zero matrix

\texttt{setupColumn}(X)

5
Approximation Methods

To compute a Gaussian Process, it is imperative to either invert an
N\times N
kernel matrix or to solve the corresponding linear systems. However, employing the Cholesky decomposition for this task usually demands
\mathcal{O}(N^{3})
time, making it impractical for large datasets (
N>10,000
). Consequently, utilizing approximation methods and leveraging the structure of the kernel are essential for efficiently computing the Gaussian Process.

In this chapter, we examine various approximation methods and introduce a new approach using Samplets. The sparsity pattern of the compressed kernel matrix suggests leveraging a sparse Cholesky decomposition, which will be a key component for achieving a fast and reliable approximation.

5.1
Overview

Several approximation and kernel learning approaches are available, and the following provides an overview of popular methods, with many of them detailed in
[
21
]
:

•

Compactly supported kernels yield a kernel that corresponds to the zero function within a subset of the domain, resulting in sparse kernel matrices. The computational reduction depends on the sparsity pattern of the matrix.

•

Low-rank approximation employs a rank
n<N
matrix to approximate the kernel matrix. Storage and inverse computation are typically reduced to
\mathcal{O}(nN)
and
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}N)
respectively. The Nyström approximation, outlined in section 8.1 of
[
21
]
, stands out as the most commonly used low-rank approximation.

•

Subset of data approximation entails selecting a subset of
n<N
points from the dataset based on a specific criterion, establishing this amount as a new dataset. Storage and inverse computation are reduced to
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
and
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
respectively.

•

Subset of Regressors exploits the fact that the prediction can be expressed as

\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_{i}k(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{i})
, as utilized in equation (
2.2.1
). It seeks to approximate the Gaussian process by selecting a subset of
n<N
functions from the
N
regressors
k(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),...,k(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{N})
based on a criterion. This reduces storage and inverse computation to
\mathcal{O}(nN)
and
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}N)
respectively.

•

Bayesian committee machine predicts the function values
\boldsymbol{f}_{*}
of
n
test points by splitting the dataset into parts
\mathcal{D}^{1},...,\mathcal{D}^{p}
. It approximates the multivariate normal distribution of
\boldsymbol{f}_{*}\,|\,\mathcal{D}_{N}
, derived in Proposition
2.1.5
, by using the distributions of
\boldsymbol{f}_{*}\,|\,\mathcal{D}^{1},...,\boldsymbol{f}_{*}\,|\,\mathcal{D}^{p}
. The computational effort reduces to
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}N)
.

•

Conjugate gradient solves the linear system
(\boldsymbol{K}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{y}
iteratively to compute the Gaussian process according to equation (
2.2.1
). If the method is terminated at iteration
n
, its complexity amounts to
\mathcal{O}(nN^{2})
.

•

Variational learning approximates the posterior distribution by selecting a subset of data, consisting of
n<N
inducing points, and minimizing the Kullbeck-Leibler divergence of the approximation from the true posterior distribution. Computation is reduced to
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}N)
and details can be found in
[
25
]
. Stochastic variational regression (SvGP) enhances this process by employing stochastic optimization techniques.

•

Expectation propagation approximates the posterior distribution by iteratively refining the approximation. It uses
n<N
points for the calculation, resulting in a cost of
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}N)
. Further details are provided in
[
27
]
.

•

Blackbox Matrix-Matrix multiplication utilizes a Lanczos algorithm to optimize the likelihood function, uses pivoted Cholesky decomposition to precondition the kernel matrix and applies a modified conjugate gradient method to compute the Gaussian Process. The key innovation lies in the parallel calculations, which reduces computational effort to
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
. For a comprehensive understanding, refer to
[
11
]
.

•

Kernel interpolation for scalable structured Gaussian Processes (KissGP) approximates the kernel function using interpolation based on
n<N
inducing points. This approach reduces computation and storage to
\mathcal{O}(N)
.

Various methods are categorized as sparse approximations because they involve removing data. Additionally, they are often termed greedy approximations, as they select points in a greedy manner rather than identifying the optimal subset of training points. This approach is influenced by combinatorial effects. Nonetheless, nearly all approximation methods follow at least one of the three ideas depicted in Figure
20
.

Figure 17:
Categories of approximation methods. Inspired by a similar Figure in
[
27
]
.

The choice of method may vary depending on the specific problem. For instance, if only prediction is of interest, the conjugate gradient method might be suitable. If the focus is on predicting for test points, the Bayesian committee machine could be more appropriate. If the entire Gaussian Process is of interest, the Nyström approximation may be employed. By using samplets, we aim to compute the entire Gaussian Process with an exact prior model while approximating the posterior functions.

Current state-of-the-art approaches for this employ preconditioned conjugate gradient methods, often combined with stochastic Lanczos quadrature. The Python library GPyTorch is committed on implementing cutting-edge algorithms and accelerating computations, including GPU support. Therefore, we will compare our samplet-based algorithms with those implemented in GPyTorch.

5.2
Gaussian Processes via Samplets

To compute the posterior functions in Proposition
2.1.5
or the marginal likelihood function in equation (
2.3.1
), we need to solve linear systems of the form

(\boldsymbol{K}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{y},

(5.2.1)

where
\boldsymbol{c}=[c_{1},...,c_{N}]^{T}
is a real-valued vector. For simplicity, we use the vector
\boldsymbol{y}=[y_{1},...,y_{N}]^{T}
on the right-hand side of (
5.2.1
), although in practice, we also need to compute solutions for different vectors. Let
\boldsymbol{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
be the orthogonal change of basis matrix from the samplet basis to the canonical basis. Then, we have the equivalent linear systems

(\boldsymbol{K}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{y}\Longleftrightarrow\boldsymbol{T}(\boldsymbol{K}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{y}\Longleftrightarrow(\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{y}.

Instead of solving the linear system for the dense matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
, which consists of many small entries, we use its compressed version
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\approx\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
to approximately compute the solution
\boldsymbol{c}
. This results in solving the perturbed linear system

(\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}=\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{y},

(5.2.2)

where
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}\approx\boldsymbol{c}
is the approximate solution. The matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}
is symmetric, and since it is a compressed version of the positive definite matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}
, we assume it remains positive definite. If this assumption fails, it is likely that the parameters
q,\eta
, or the additional threshold for the matrix entries were poorly selected. It is also common to use
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}>\sigma^{2}
to enforce positive definiteness. As suggested in Remark
3.3.10
, it is recommended to use
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}\sim h_{X_{N},\Omega}^{\tau-d/2}
when working with a
\tau
-smooth kernel.

The perturbed linear system is, under suitable conditions, faster to solve than the original system in equation (
5.2.1
). Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open and convex set,
k_{\nu,\ell}
a Matérn kernel and
(X_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of quasi-uniform points in
\Omega
. In chapter
4
, we established the following results:

•

\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}
is a sparse matrix with
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
non-zero entries.

•

\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}
can be computed in
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
time, requiring
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
storage.

•

\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{y}
can be computed via the fast samplet transform in
\mathcal{O}(N)
operations.

•

Given
\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}
, then
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}=\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})
can be computed via the inverse fast samplet transform in
\mathcal{O}(N)
operations.

This implies that for large
N
, the primary computational effort is in solving equation (
5.2.2
) for
\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}
. The method employed should take advantage of the sparsity pattern of the matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}
. Typically, a sparse Cholesky decomposition or a modified conjugate gradient method would be the initial approaches to consider. If such a method requires
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
operations, then the overall cost of approximating the Gaussian Process would also be
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
. However, accurately determining the computational effort of these methods is challenging due to fill-in during the process. Given the Cholesky decomposition
\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}=\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}
, we formulate Algorithm
9
to compute
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}\approx\boldsymbol{c}
.

Algorithm 9

Perturbed linear system

Data:

Cluster tree
\mathcal{T}
, Cholesky
\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}=\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}
, vector
\boldsymbol{y}
,
(\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}^{v})_{v\in P\backslash\mathcal{L(P)}}

Result:

Approximate solution
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}\approx\boldsymbol{c}
of the system
(\boldsymbol{K}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{y}

Function

SolvePerturbedSystem(
\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{y}
)
:

\boldsymbol{Ty}=\texttt{SampletTransform}(\boldsymbol{y})

\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}=\boldsymbol{L}^{T}\backslash(\boldsymbol{L}\backslash\boldsymbol{Ty})

\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}=\texttt{InverseTransform}(\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})

return

\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}

end

Using Algorithm
8
to compute
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
, a sparse Cholesky decomposition to calculate
\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}=\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}
and Algorithm
9
to solve the corresponding linear systems, the computation of the posterior functions in Proposition
2.1.5
becomes straightforward. Additionally, by utilizing the approximation

\displaystyle\text{det}(\boldsymbol{K}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})

\displaystyle=\text{det}(\boldsymbol{T}(\boldsymbol{K}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})\boldsymbol{T}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\text{det}(\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})\approx\text{det}(\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T})=\text{det}^{2}(\boldsymbol{L})=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}L_{i,i}\right)^{2},

we can also compute the log marginal likelihood in equation (
2.3.1
) as

\log p(\boldsymbol{y}\,|\,X)\approx-\dfrac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{y}^{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log L_{i,i}-\dfrac{N}{2}\log 2\pi

(5.2.3)

in
\mathcal{O}(N)
operations. To learn the hyperparameters of the Gaussian Process, it is common to optimize this likelihood function through gradient descent methods, which requires evaluation of equation (
2.3.2
). Let
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
be a hyperparameter of the Gaussian Process, then we approximate

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{c}^{T}\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{c}^{T}\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{T}\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{c}=(\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{c})^{T}\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{c}\approx(\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})^{T}\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}

and estimate

\displaystyle\text{tr}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{-1}\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{T}\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{T}\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)=\text{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{T}\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{T}\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{tr}\left(\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\right)^{-1}\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\right)\approx\text{tr}\left(\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\right)^{-1}\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\right),

where we have
\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}=\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}
. In
[
15
]
, the authors demonstrated that under certain assumptions, the involved inverse matrix has only
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
non-zero entries and for large
N
, we have

\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\right)^{-1}\approx\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\right)^{-1}\qquad\text{in the same sense as}\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\approx\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}.

They also propose an algorithm for computing this inverse using the Cholesky decomposition, but a direct computation is generally inefficient. Instead, let
\boldsymbol{z}_{1},...,\boldsymbol{z}_{t}
be random variables with
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{N})
, and apply the stochastic trace estimation

\displaystyle\text{tr}\left(\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\right)^{-1}\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{z}_{i}^{T}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\right)^{-1}\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle\approx\dfrac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\left(\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\right)^{T}\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}.

This estimate is unbiased and widely used (see e.g.
[
11
]
), as it requires only a small number
t
for a reliable approximation. By leveraging the precomputed Cholesky decomposition, we can efficiently solve the linear systems. Consequently, we compute the derivative of the log marginal likelihood function from equation (
2.3.2
) as

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta}\log p(\boldsymbol{y}\,|\,X)\approx\dfrac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})^{T}\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}-\dfrac{1}{2t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\left(\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\right)^{T}\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}.

It is not obvious why this approximation should be computationally faster than calculating the gradient using equation (
2.3.2
). However, with an understanding of the involved sparsity pattern and the structure of the derivatives, the time savings become clear. Since the Cholesky decomposition
\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}=\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
typically reflects the sparsity pattern of
\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
, we expect the computational cost of solving the linear systems to be significantly less than the usual
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
operations. Specifically, because the compressed matrix contains only
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
non-zero entries, we anticipate the cost also to be
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
.

The derivative of
\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}
with respect to the hyperparameter
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
often inherits the kernel matrix
\boldsymbol{K}
or a variation of it. For example, consider the Matérn kernel
s^{2}k_{\nu,\ell}
with
\ell,s^{2}>0
and
\nu=n+1/2
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
. In this case, the hyperparameters are
\{\ell,s^{2},\sigma^{2}\}
, and the derivatives are given by

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\ell}=\dfrac{s^{2}\sqrt{2\nu}}{\ell^{2}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{K}}-\dfrac{1}{\ell}\boldsymbol{K}\qquad\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial s^{2}}=\dfrac{\boldsymbol{K}}{s^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\sigma^{2}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{N},

where

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{K}}:=\big[\|\boldsymbol{x}_{i}-\boldsymbol{x}_{j}\|_{2}k_{\nu,\ell}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{x}_{j})\big]_{1\leq i,j\leq N}.

As before, the matrix
\boldsymbol{K}
can be compressed using samplets. The function

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\mapsto\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\|_{2}k_{\nu,\ell}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})

(5.2.4)

is symmetric, but generally not positive definite, meaning
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{K}}
is not a kernel matrix. However, since the theory of samplets is not restricted to kernel functions, we can still compress
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{K}}
. Furthermore, since Proposition
4.3.2
holds more generally for symmetric and holomorphic functions, the function in (
5.2.4
) is sufficiently asymptotically smooth to ensure that Theorem
4.3.5
applies and we have

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\approx\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\qquad\text{in the same sense as}\qquad\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\approx\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}.

Aside from that,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
consists of
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
non-zero entries. Using Algorithm
8
, we require
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
operations to compute the derivatives

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\ell}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}=\dfrac{s^{2}\sqrt{2\nu}}{\ell^{2}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}-\dfrac{1}{\ell}\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\qquad\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial s^{2}}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}=\dfrac{\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}}{s^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}=\boldsymbol{I}_{N},

where
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
has already been computed when solving the linear system in equation (
5.2.1
). With efficient coding, it should be possible to compute
\boldsymbol{K}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}
in a single process.

The main computational effort involved in approximating the derivative of the log marginal likelihood lies in solving the linear systems. If this takes
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
operations, the effort of evaluating the gradient is also
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
. Algorithm
10
outlines the procedure for approximating a Gaussian Process via Samplets. Based on the computational cost of the Cholesky decomposition, and the sparsity pattern of
\boldsymbol{L}
, the algorithm requires
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
operations at best.

Algorithm 10

SampletsGP: Gaussian Process via Samplets

Data:

Data
\mathcal{D}_{N}=\{X_{train},\boldsymbol{y}_{train}\}
, kernel
k
, initial hyperparameters
\theta_{N,1}
, vanishing moments
q
, compression parameter
\eta
, compression threshold
\tau_{comp}
, gradient based optimizer, optimization steps
n_{\text{steps}}

Result:

Posterior Process
\mathcal{GP}(m^{\prime},k^{\prime})
ready for prediction

Function

TrainGP(
X_{\text{train}},\boldsymbol{y}_{\text{train}},n_{\text{steps}}
)
:

store ST =
\texttt{SampletTree}(X_{\text{train}},q)

for

i=1

to

n_{steps}

do

\boldsymbol{K}_{\eta}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\texttt{SampletKernelCompressor}(\text{ST},k(\theta_{N,i}),X_{train},\eta,\tau_{comp})

\boldsymbol{L}=\texttt{SparseCholesky}(\boldsymbol{K}_{\eta}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\sigma_{N,i}^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})

\boldsymbol{Ty}_{train}=\texttt{SampletTransform}(\boldsymbol{y}_{train})

\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}=\boldsymbol{L}^{T}\backslash(\boldsymbol{L}\backslash\boldsymbol{Ty}_{train})

[\boldsymbol{z}_{1},...,\boldsymbol{z}_{50}]=\texttt{Random.standard\_normal}(\text{size}=N,\text{dimension}=50)

[\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1},...,\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{50}]=\boldsymbol{L}^{T}\backslash(\boldsymbol{L}\backslash[\boldsymbol{z}_{1},...,\boldsymbol{z}_{50}])

\nabla=\{\}

foreach

\text{hyperparameter }\vartheta

do

D=\dfrac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})^{T}\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\boldsymbol{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}-\dfrac{1}{100}\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{50}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}^{T}\left(\dfrac{\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}

\nabla=\nabla\cup\{D\}

end foreach

store
\theta_{N,i+1}=\texttt{Update}(optimizer,\nabla,\theta_{N,i})

end for

\boldsymbol{K}_{\eta}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\texttt{SampletKernelCompressor}(\text{ST},k(\theta_{N,n_{steps}}),X_{train},\eta,\tau_{comp})

store
\boldsymbol{L}=\texttt{SparseCholesky}(\boldsymbol{K}_{\eta}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\sigma_{N,n_{steps}}^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N})

store
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}=\texttt{SolvePerturbedSystem}(\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{y}_{\text{train}})

store the models log-likelihood
-\dfrac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{y}_{train}^{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log L_{i,i}-\dfrac{N}{2}\log 2\pi

end

Function

PredictGP(
X_{\text{pred}}
)
:

cov=k(\theta_{N,n_{steps}})

\boldsymbol{K}_{1}=cov(X_{pred},X_{train})

\boldsymbol{K}_{2}=cov(X_{pred},X_{pred})

\boldsymbol{y}_{pred}=\boldsymbol{K}_{1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}

\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}=\boldsymbol{L}\backslash(\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{K}_{1}^{T})

\boldsymbol{K}_{pred}=\boldsymbol{K}_{2}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}^{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}

return

\boldsymbol{y}_{pred},\boldsymbol{K}_{pred}

end

5.3
Bayesian Optimization via Samplets

To optimize a black-box function
f:[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
through Bayesian optimization, we employ Gaussian Processes as surrogate models and use Thompson samples as acquisition functions.

For large
N
, we approximate the Gaussian Process using samplets, following the procedure in Algorithm
10
. Each GP requires constructing a samplet tree based on the
N
observations. However, when only a few new observations are added, it is inefficient to build a completely new samplet tree. Instead, we update the existing tree by incorporating new observations into a single cluster at the highest level of the tree. This strategy ensures that only
\mathcal{O}(\log N)
samplets need to be updated. Specifically, updating the samplet tree takes
\mathcal{O}(\log N)
operations, instead of the
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
operations required for building a new one. Since only a small number of samplets are modified, updating the corresponding entries in the compressed kernel matrix is also more efficient than computing the entire matrix. Nevertheless, the computational gain is mostly in constants, as the update still requires
\mathcal{O}(N\log N)
operations. To balance efficiency with accuracy, it is beneficial to rebuild the samplet tree periodically, for example, after every
1,000
new observations, to fully leverage the potential of samplets.

One advantage of using Thompson sampling is the ability to select multiple new observations before constructing the next surrogate model. For large
N
, it becomes inefficient and not worthwhile to compute the surrogate model after selecting each individual point. Instead, we determine
100
new observations at once before computing the next GP. To maintain a quasi-uniform distribution of points, we apply the
\gamma
-stabilized algorithm framework as described in
[
5
]
. Let
k
denote a stationary and
\tau
-smooth kernel with
\tau>d/2+1
. For a Thompson sample
a:[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\gamma\in(0,1]
, the next point is chosen as

\boldsymbol{x}_{\text{next}}=\underset{\boldsymbol{x}\in[0,1]^{d}_{N,\gamma}}{\argmax}\,a(\boldsymbol{x}),

where
[0,1]^{d}_{N,\gamma}:=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in[0,1]^{d}\ |\ k^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\geq\gamma\|k^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{d})}\}
and
k^{\prime}
is the posterior kernel function after observing
N
points. We use Algorithm
1
to generate Thompson samples, with each sample based on
100d
candidate points. Once
N_{0}-1
points have been selected, the final point is chosen to maximize the posterior mean function
m^{\prime}
, leading to

|f(\boldsymbol{x}^{*})-f(\boldsymbol{x}_{N_{0}})|\leq|f(\boldsymbol{x}^{*})-m^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{x}^{*})-f(\boldsymbol{x}_{N_{0}})-m^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{x}_{N_{0}})|\leq 2\|f-m^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{d})}.

Since we maintain a quasi-uniform distribution of points, Theorems
3.3.3
and
3.3.5
guarantee convergence as
N_{0}
increases. If the smoothness of the function is correctly estimated by the kernel, we achieve a convergence rate of
\mathcal{O}(N_{0}^{-\tau_{f}/d+1/2})
in the interpolation setting and
\mathcal{O}(N_{0}^{-\tau_{f}/(2\tau_{f}+d)+d/2})
in the regression setting, with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{d})}
. Algorithm
11
details the procedure for performing Bayesian optimization using samplets and incorporating Thompson sampling in conjunction with the
\gamma
-stabilized algorithm framework.

Algorithm 11

Bayesian Optimization via Samplets

Data:

Black-box function
f
, optimization steps
N_{0}
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
, Data from Algorithm
10

Result:

Estimate of the optimum
\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{*},f(\boldsymbol{x}^{*})\right)

Function

SampletsBO(
N_{0},\gamma
)
:

X_{train},\boldsymbol{y}_{train}=\{\},\{\}

for

i=1

to

N_{0}-1

do

if

i
is a multiple of
100

then

\texttt{TrainGP}(X_{train},\boldsymbol{y}_{train},n_{steps})

end if

X=\texttt{Random.rand}(\text{size}=100d,\text{dimension}=d)

\boldsymbol{m}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{K}^{\prime}=\texttt{PredictGP}(X)

\boldsymbol{L}=\texttt{Cholesky}(\boldsymbol{K}^{\prime})

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\texttt{Random.standard\_normal}(\text{size}=100d)

[a(\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),...,a(\boldsymbol{x}_{100d})]=\boldsymbol{m}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
